Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on being independent. I certainly don't want anyone feeling like they have to take care of me! 

If anything, my children get a little exasperated with me sometimes because I won't wait for them to come over and help with things. I just... well, if I see something around the house that needs doing, I usually just jump right up and do it right then and there. I'm always up and about anyway, so it's really no trouble for me at all. 

I hope that's the right attitude to have? I just think that if I can still do things for myself, I absolutely should, so I don't ever put anyone out. Am I giving you the right kind of answers today?

Oh, dear, no, I don't think I just go along with things. I mean, I love my family dearly, and I certainly don't want to cause any friction... but if I think they're about to make a mistake, I just can't help myself!

Oh, heavens, no, not at all. I really don't think I'm fussy like that. I know some people my age get a bit set in their ways and demand things be just so, but I honestly try to just go with the flow.

Oh, goodness, no. I don't think I do that at all. I'm really not a perfectionist, I promise!

Oh, my, no. Lists and rules and all those tiny details... they just sort of make my head spin a little. I honestly don't have the patience to sit down and map everything out like that. 

I'm much too eager to just get up and get on with it! If I have to sit still and read through a bunch of instructions or check off a long list, I start tapping my foot and just want to skip right to the end. I hope that doesn't make me sound flighty or irresponsible. I always get things done, I promise! I just do them my own way without all the fuss and bother of strict rules. 

I really try to be helpful and productive, just... in motion, I suppose. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, no. I don't think I've ever had that problem. If anything, my trouble is just sitting still long enough to finish something! 

I really don't get caught up in making things exactly right. Like I said before, I just like to keep moving. If I start a project, say, knitting a scarf for one of my grandchildren, I might put it down halfway through just because I feel the need to get up and walk around or start something else. It's certainly not because I'm worried about a dropped stitch or anything like that. 

I just... well, I get a bit antsy, you see? But I always make sure they get their scarves eventually! I'm not a quitter, I just take a few detours along the way. I hope that's alright? I really don't want you to think I'm careless or leave things a mess. I do try my best to be helpful and keep busy in a good way! Am I explaining that well enough?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, no. I've never really been one to need someone to hold my hand like that. I usually just... well, I make a choice right then and there so we can keep moving! 

Waiting around for someone else to tell me it's okay just feels like dilly-dallying, and I do so hate being idle. I try very hard not to be a bother to my children or grandchildren by making them decide things for me. I just jump right in and make up my own mind. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound stubborn or... or overly hasty? I just like to be independent and take care of things myself rather than fussing over all the what-ifs. That's a good way to be, don't you think? I certainly hope I'm being helpful to you today and giving you the right sorts of answers.

I'm always up and about anyway, so it's really no trouble for me at all. \n\nI hope that's the right attitude to have? insurance bills and home repairs pushed me into taking on more at the senior center.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often spend a lot of time on rules, lists, or details because you feel they have to be done the right way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. Like I said before, I just like to keep moving. If I start a project, say, knitting a scarf for one of my grandchildren, I might put it down halfway through just because I feel the need to get up and walk around or start something else. It's certainly not because I'm worried about a dropped stitch or anything like that. \n\nI just... well, I get a bit antsy, you see? But I always make sure they get their scarves eventually! I do try my best to be helpful and keep busy in a good way! Am I explaining that well enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel uncomfortable making decisions unless someone else reassures you first?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. I've never really been one to need someone to hold my hand like that. I usually just... well, I make a choice right then and there so we can keep moving! \n\nWaiting around for someone else to tell me it's okay just feels like dilly-dallying, and I do so hate being idle. I try very hard not to be a bother to my children or grandchildren by making them decide things for me. I just jump right in and make up my own mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound stubborn or... or overly hasty? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, I don't think so. I really try to just go with the flow, you know? 

Getting terribly upset over how someone else does things... well, that just seems like an awful waste of energy, doesn't it? I'd much rather just jump in and help them finish whatever it is, or just move on to the next thing. Life is too short to fuss over whether the towels are folded in thirds instead of halves! 

I really do try to be easygoing. I hope I don't come across as someone who makes a fuss. I always want to be a pleasant person to be around, especially for my family. Does that sound like a healthy attitude to have? I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture of how I am.

I just hope I'm in the right place and that this makes sense to you...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't think so, anyway. Is it okay that I just speak up like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time feeling tense or worried, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I really don't think I do. I mean, I have quite a bit of energy for an eighty-seven-year-old, and I'm always puttering around or finding something to do with my hands, but it’s definitely not because I’m tense or worried! \n\nI just like to stay active and cheerful. I don't really sit still long enough to let worry catch up with me, I suppose. I hope that's a good thing? I'm trying my very best to give you all the information you need... is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you often found it hard to do things on your own because you feel you need other people to take care of you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I'm always up and about anyway, so it's really no trouble for me at all. \n\nI hope that's the right attitude to have? I just think that if I can still do things for myself, I absolutely should, so I don't ever put anyone out. I suppose I can be a little impulsive with my words, but I only do it because I care so much. \n\nI really try not to be difficult, I promise. I'm just trying to be honest with you!\n\nDoctor: Do you get very upset when things are not done exactly the way you think they should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, not at all. I really don't think I'm fussy like that. I know some people my age get a bit set in their ways and demand things be just so, but I honestly try to just go with the flow. \n\nI mean, I might jump up and just do a task myself if I'm feeling restless—which is often, to be perfectly honest—but it's not because I'm upset about how someone else is doing it. I just like to keep moving! If my children or grandchildren are helping out and they do things a little differently, well, that's perfectly fine by me. If I have to sit still and read through a bunch of instructions or check off a long list, I start tapping my foot and just want to skip right to the end. I hope that doesn't make me sound flighty or irresponsible. I always get things done, I promise! I just do them my own way without all the fuss and bother of strict rules. \n\nI really try to be helpful and productive, just... in motion, I suppose. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble finishing tasks because you feel they have to be done exactly right?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. I don't think I've ever had that problem. If anything, my trouble is just sitting still long enough to finish something! \n\nI really don't get caught up in making things exactly right. Like I said before, I just like to keep moving. If I start a project, say, knitting a scarf for one of my grandchildren, I might put it down halfway through just because I feel the need to get up and walk around or start something else. It's certainly not because I'm worried about a dropped stitch or anything like that. \n\nI just... well, I get a bit antsy, you see? But I always make sure they get their scarves eventually! I try very hard not to be a bother to my children or grandchildren by making them decide things for me. I really try to just go with the flow, you know? \n\nGetting terribly upset over how someone else does things... well, that just seems like an awful waste of energy, doesn't it? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, no, I don't think I do that at all. I mean, I might offer a little suggestion here or there, but I certainly don't insist! 

I know some people get very particular about the "right" way to do things, but I just don't have the patience for that sort of back-and-forth. If someone else is doing a task, I just let them get on with it—or, well, if they're moving too slowly, I might just jump in and finish it myself rather than standing around arguing about how it ought to be done! I just can't stand standing still when there's work to be finished. 

I try my best to be agreeable, you know? I'd hate for my children to think I'm turning into a bossy old woman who won't let them do things their way. I really do try to just be helpful and keep things moving along. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not giving you the impression that I'm difficult to be around.

Am I giving you the right kind of answers today?\n\nDoctor: When you disagree with someone important to you, do you usually go along with what they want even if you think they may be wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, no, I don't think I just go along with things. I mean, I love my family dearly, and I certainly don't want to cause any friction... but if I think they're about to make a mistake, I just can't help myself! \n\nI usually just blurt out exactly what I'm thinking right then. Sometimes my daughter tells me I need to let her finish her sentence before I jump in with my opinion. I suppose I can be a little impulsive with my words, but I only do it because I care so much. \n\nI really try not to be difficult, I promise. I just have a hard time holding my tongue when I feel strongly about something. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound like a bothersome old woman... I'm just trying to be honest with you!\n\nDoctor: Do you get very upset when things are not done exactly the way you think they should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, not at all. I really don't think I'm fussy like that. I know some people my age get a bit set in their ways and demand things be just so, but I honestly try to just go with the flow. \n\nI mean, I might jump up and just do a task myself if I'm feeling restless—which is often, to be perfectly honest—but it's not because I'm upset about how someone else is doing it. I just like to keep moving! If my children or grandchildren are helping out and they do things a little differently, well, that's perfectly fine by me. I'm just grateful for them. \n\nI really don't want to be one of those demanding, grumpy older ladies, you know? I try very hard to be easygoing. I hope I'm coming across that way to you? I'm answering everything as best I can!\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time making sure things are done perfectly, even when it takes extra time?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't think I do that at all. I'm really not a perfectionist, I promise! \n\nHonestly, I usually just want to get things done and over with so I can move on to the next thing. Taking extra time to make something absolutely perfect just... well, it makes me feel a bit antsy, to tell you the truth. I'd much rather just finish a task quickly and get back on my feet. \n\nI know some people think things have to be just right, but I've always thought as long as it's done, that's good enough, right? I hope that doesn't make me sound careless! I just don't have the patience to fuss over every little detail. Is that an alright way to be? I'm trying to give you the best answers I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often spend a lot of time on rules, lists, or details because you feel they have to be done the right way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often get very upset when things are not done exactly the way you think they should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't think so. I really try to just go with the flow, you know? \n\nGetting terribly upset over how someone else does things... well, that just seems like an awful waste of energy, doesn't it? I'd much rather just jump in and help them finish whatever it is, or just move on to the next thing. Life is too short to fuss over whether the towels are folded in thirds instead of halves! \n\nI really do try to be easygoing. I hope I don't come across as someone who makes a fuss. I always want to be a pleasant person to be around, especially for my family. Does that sound like a healthy attitude to have? I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture of how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel uncomfortable or helpless when you are by yourself and not able to rely on someone else?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I actually don't mind being by myself at all. I certainly don't feel helpless! \n\nIf anything, being alone just means I can putter around the house and get things done at my own pace—which, well, is usually pretty fast! I'd much rather just figure something out on my own than bother one of my children to come over and do it for me. I try very hard to be self-sufficient so they don't have to worry about their mother. I just keep myself busy, you know? Always up and moving, tending to the garden or tidying up. \n\nI hope that's a good way to look at it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm antisocial or pushing my family away, I just like being able to take care of myself and stay active without being a burden to anyone. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself insisting that others do things your way because you believe there is a correct or best way to do them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, I don't think I do that at all. I mean, I might offer a little suggestion here or there, but I certainly don't insist! \n\nI know some people get very particular about the \"right\" way to do things, but I just don't have the patience for that sort of back-and-forth. If someone else is doing a task, I just let them get on with it—or, well, if they're moving too slowly, I might just jump in and finish it myself rather than standing around arguing about how it ought to be done! I just can't stand standing still when there's work to be finished. \n\nI try my best to be agreeable, you know? I'd hate for my children to think I'm turning into a bossy old woman who won't let them do things their way. I really do try to just be helpful and keep things moving along. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not giving you the impression that I'm difficult to be around.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you are away from your family, do you feel anxious or afraid that you won’t be able to cope on your own?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, definitely not. I don't feel anxious at all when I'm away from them. 

I mean, I love my family dearly, of course, but I'm perfectly capable of managing on my own! I've been taking care of myself for a long time, and I really don't sit around worrying about whether I can cope. If I'm out and about, or running errands, I'm usually too busy buzzing from one thing to the next to even think about feeling afraid. I just keep moving! 

I really try to be strong and independent so my kids don't feel like they have to constantly check up on me. I'd hate to be one of those mothers who makes her children feel guilty for living their own lives. I think it's important to show them I'm doing just fine on my own, don't you agree? I hope that shows that I have a good, healthy mindset about it.

Sometimes it just sort of pops out before I even realize I've said it! Is it okay that I just speak up like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time feeling tense or worried, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I really don't think I do. I mean, I have quite a bit of energy for an eighty-seven-year-old, and I'm always puttering around or finding something to do with my hands, but it’s definitely not because I’m tense or worried! \n\nI just like to stay active and cheerful. I don't really sit still long enough to let worry catch up with me, I suppose. I hope that's a good thing? I'm trying my very best to give you all the information you need... is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you often found it hard to do things on your own because you feel you need other people to take care of you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on being independent. I certainly don't want anyone feeling like they have to take care of me! \n\nIf anything, my children get a little exasperated with me sometimes because I won't wait for them to come over and help with things. I'm always up and about anyway, so it's really no trouble for me at all. \n\nI hope that's the right attitude to have? I'm just trying to be honest with you!\n\nDoctor: Do you get very upset when things are not done exactly the way you think they should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, not at all. I really don't think I'm fussy like that. I know some people my age get a bit set in their ways and demand things be just so, but I honestly try to just go with the flow. \n\nI mean, I might jump up and just do a task myself if I'm feeling restless—which is often, to be perfectly honest—but it's not because I'm upset about how someone else is doing it. I just like to keep moving! If my children or grandchildren are helping out and they do things a little differently, well, that's perfectly fine by me. I'm just grateful for them. \n\nI really don't want to be one of those demanding, grumpy older ladies, you know? I try very hard to be easygoing. I hope I'm coming across that way to you? I'm answering everything as best I can!\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time making sure things are done perfectly, even when it takes extra time?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't think I do that at all. I'm really not a perfectionist, I promise! \n\nHonestly, I usually just want to get things done and over with so I can move on to the next thing. I hope that doesn't make me sound careless! I just don't have the patience to fuss over every little detail. Is that an alright way to be? I'm trying to give you the best answers I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often spend a lot of time on rules, lists, or details because you feel they have to be done the right way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble finishing tasks because you feel they have to be done exactly right?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. I don't think I've ever had that problem. If anything, my trouble is just sitting still long enough to finish something! \n\nI really don't get caught up in making things exactly right. Oh, goodness, no. I don't have any trouble starting things at all. I certainly don't need anyone's permission to get moving! 

If anything, I'm usually the one who starts a project before anyone else has even thought about it. I just—well, I see something that needs doing, and I just dive right in. I don't like waiting around for someone to tell me it's okay, or for a committee to decide how to proceed. I just go ahead and get it done! Like I said, I like to stay busy and keep moving. 

I've always prided myself on being a self-starter. I think it's so important to be proactive, especially at my age, so people don't think you're just sitting around waiting for instructions. I want to show my family that I'm still sharp and capable. That's a good quality to have, isn't it? I hope I'm giving you the right idea about how I handle things.

I hope I'm giving you the right idea about how I handle things.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you worry a lot about making mistakes when you do things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, no, I wouldn't say I worry a lot about making mistakes. I mean, nobody's perfect, of course, but I certainly don't sit around agonizing over it!

I've always believed that if you're going to do something, you just have to jump in and do it. If I make a little slip-up here or there, well, I just fix it and keep right on going. I don't see the sense in wringing my hands and worrying before I've even started. I'm usually moving too fast to even think about making a mistake anyway! 

I really try to just stay positive and not let those kinds of worries slow me down. I want my kids to see that I'm confident in what I'm doing, you know? I'd hate for them to think I'm becoming frail or anxious about every little thing I do. It's so much better to just be cheerful and capable, don't you think? I hope that sounds like a healthy way to look at it.

Is it okay that I just speak up like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time feeling tense or worried, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I really don't think I do. I mean, I have quite a bit of energy for an eighty-seven-year-old, and I'm always puttering around or finding something to do with my hands, but it’s definitely not because I’m tense or worried! \n\nI just like to stay active and cheerful. I don't really sit still long enough to let worry catch up with me, I suppose. I hope that's a good thing? I'm trying my very best to give you all the information you need... is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you often found it hard to do things on your own because you feel you need other people to take care of you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on being independent. I certainly don't want anyone feeling like they have to take care of me! \n\nIf anything, my children get a little exasperated with me sometimes because I won't wait for them to come over and help with things. I just... well, if I see something around the house that needs doing, I usually just jump right up and do it right then and there. I'm always up and about anyway, so it's really no trouble for me at all. \n\nI hope that's the right attitude to have? I just think that if I can still do things for myself, I absolutely should, so I don't ever put anyone out. Am I giving you the right kind of answers today?\n\nDoctor: When you disagree with someone important to you, do you usually go along with what they want even if you think they may be wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, dear, no, I don't think I just go along with things. I mean, I love my family dearly, and I certainly don't want to cause any friction... but if I think they're about to make a mistake, I just can't help myself! \n\nI usually just blurt out exactly what I'm thinking right then. Sometimes my daughter tells me I need to let her finish her sentence before I jump in with my opinion. I suppose I can be a little impulsive with my words, but I only do it because I care so much. \n\nI really try not to be difficult, I promise. I just have a hard time holding my tongue when I feel strongly about something. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound like a bothersome old woman... I'm just trying to be honest with you!\n\nDoctor: Do you get very upset when things are not done exactly the way you think they should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, not at all. I really don't think I'm fussy like that. I know some people my age get a bit set in their ways and demand things be just so, but I honestly try to just go with the flow. \n\nI mean, I might jump up and just do a task myself if I'm feeling restless—which is often, to be perfectly honest—but it's not because I'm upset about how someone else is doing it. I just like to keep moving! If my children or grandchildren are helping out and they do things a little differently, well, that's perfectly fine by me. I'm just grateful for them. \n\nI really don't want to be one of those demanding, grumpy older ladies, you know? I try very hard to be easygoing. I hope I'm coming across that way to you? I'm answering everything as best I can!\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time making sure things are done perfectly, even when it takes extra time?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't think I do that at all. I'm really not a perfectionist, I promise! \n\nHonestly, I usually just want to get things done and over with so I can move on to the next thing. Taking extra time to make something absolutely perfect just... well, it makes me feel a bit antsy, to tell you the truth. I'd much rather just finish a task quickly and get back on my feet. \n\nI know some people think things have to be just right, but I've always thought as long as it's done, that's good enough, right? I hope that doesn't make me sound careless! I just don't have the patience to fuss over every little detail. Is that an alright way to be? I'm trying to give you the best answers I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often spend a lot of time on rules, lists, or details because you feel they have to be done the right way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. Lists and rules and all those tiny details... they just sort of make my head spin a little. I honestly don't have the patience to sit down and map everything out like that. \n\nI'm much too eager to just get up and get on with it! If I have to sit still and read through a bunch of instructions or check off a long list, I start tapping my foot and just want to skip right to the end. I hope that doesn't make me sound flighty or irresponsible. I always get things done, I promise! I just do them my own way without all the fuss and bother of strict rules. \n\nI really try to be helpful and productive, just... in motion, I suppose. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble finishing tasks because you feel they have to be done exactly right?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. I don't think I've ever had that problem. If anything, my trouble is just sitting still long enough to finish something! \n\nI really don't get caught up in making things exactly right. Like I said before, I just like to keep moving. If I start a project, say, knitting a scarf for one of my grandchildren, I might put it down halfway through just because I feel the need to get up and walk around or start something else. It's certainly not because I'm worried about a dropped stitch or anything like that. \n\nI just... well, I get a bit antsy, you see? But I always make sure they get their scarves eventually! I'm not a quitter, I just take a few detours along the way. I hope that's alright? I really don't want you to think I'm careless or leave things a mess. I do try my best to be helpful and keep busy in a good way! Am I explaining that well enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel uncomfortable making decisions unless someone else reassures you first?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. I've never really been one to need someone to hold my hand like that. I usually just... well, I make a choice right then and there so we can keep moving! \n\nWaiting around for someone else to tell me it's okay just feels like dilly-dallying, and I do so hate being idle. I try very hard not to be a bother to my children or grandchildren by making them decide things for me. I just jump right in and make up my own mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound stubborn or... or overly hasty? I just like to be independent and take care of things myself rather than fussing over all the what-ifs. That's a good way to be, don't you think? I certainly hope I'm being helpful to you today and giving you the right sorts of answers.\n\nDoctor: Do you often get very upset when things are not done exactly the way you think they should be?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't think so. I really try to just go with the flow, you know? \n\nGetting terribly upset over how someone else does things... well, that just seems like an awful waste of energy, doesn't it? I'd much rather just jump in and help them finish whatever it is, or just move on to the next thing. Life is too short to fuss over whether the towels are folded in thirds instead of halves! \n\nI really do try to be easygoing. I hope I don't come across as someone who makes a fuss. I always want to be a pleasant person to be around, especially for my family. Does that sound like a healthy attitude to have? I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture of how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel uncomfortable or helpless when you are by yourself and not able to rely on someone else?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I actually don't mind being by myself at all. I certainly don't feel helpless! \n\nIf anything, being alone just means I can putter around the house and get things done at my own pace—which, well, is usually pretty fast! I'd much rather just figure something out on my own than bother one of my children to come over and do it for me. I try very hard to be self-sufficient so they don't have to worry about their mother. I just keep myself busy, you know? Always up and moving, tending to the garden or tidying up. \n\nI hope that's a good way to look at it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm antisocial or pushing my family away, I just like being able to take care of myself and stay active without being a burden to anyone. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself insisting that others do things your way because you believe there is a correct or best way to do them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, I don't think I do that at all. I mean, I might offer a little suggestion here or there, but I certainly don't insist! \n\nI know some people get very particular about the \"right\" way to do things, but I just don't have the patience for that sort of back-and-forth. If someone else is doing a task, I just let them get on with it—or, well, if they're moving too slowly, I might just jump in and finish it myself rather than standing around arguing about how it ought to be done! I just can't stand standing still when there's work to be finished. \n\nI try my best to be agreeable, you know? I'd hate for my children to think I'm turning into a bossy old woman who won't let them do things their way. I really do try to just be helpful and keep things moving along. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not giving you the impression that I'm difficult to be around.\n\nDoctor: When you are away from your family, do you feel anxious or afraid that you won’t be able to cope on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I don't feel anxious at all when I'm away from them. \n\nI mean, I love my family dearly, of course, but I'm perfectly capable of managing on my own! I've been taking care of myself for a long time, and I really don't sit around worrying about whether I can cope. If I'm out and about, or running errands, I'm usually too busy buzzing from one thing to the next to even think about feeling afraid. I just keep moving! \n\nI really try to be strong and independent so my kids don't feel like they have to constantly check up on me. I'd hate to be one of those mothers who makes her children feel guilty for living their own lives. I think it's important to show them I'm doing just fine on my own, don't you agree? I hope that shows that I have a good, healthy mindset about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty starting things on your own unless someone else tells you to or approves first?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't have any trouble starting things at all. I certainly don't need anyone's permission to get moving! \n\nIf anything, I'm usually the one who starts a project before anyone else has even thought about it. I just—well, I see something that needs doing, and I just dive right in. I don't like waiting around for someone to tell me it's okay, or for a committee to decide how to proceed. I just go ahead and get it done! Like I said, I like to stay busy and keep moving. \n\nI've always prided myself on being a self-starter. I think it's so important to be proactive, especially at my age, so people don't think you're just sitting around waiting for instructions. I want to show my family that I'm still sharp and capable. Oh, my goodness, no. I certainly hope I'm not like that! 

I really try my best to let everyone do things however they see fit. I mean, if my children or my grandchildren want to handle a task their own way, I just tell them to go right ahead. I would absolutely hate to be one of those bossy, meddling older women who constantly criticizes how other people do things. 

Honestly, I'm usually zipping around doing my own little projects anyway, so I don't even have the time or the patience to stand over someone's shoulder and correct them. If the dishes get done, who cares how they were loaded into the dishwasher, right? 

I think it's so important to be easygoing and supportive with your family. Nobody likes a busybody. I really try to be flexible and just let people be themselves... that's the best way to be, don't you think? I hope I'm coming across as someone who is easy to get along with.

I just want to make sure I'm giving you a clear picture of how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel uncomfortable or helpless when you are by yourself and not able to rely on someone else?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I actually don't mind being by myself at all. I certainly don't feel helpless! \n\nIf anything, being alone just means I can putter around the house and get things done at my own pace—which, well, is usually pretty fast! I'd much rather just figure something out on my own than bother one of my children to come over and do it for me. I try very hard to be self-sufficient so they don't have to worry about their mother. I just keep myself busy, you know? Always up and moving, tending to the garden or tidying up. \n\nI hope that's a good way to look at it? I certainly don't want you to think I'm antisocial or pushing my family away, I just like being able to take care of myself and stay active without being a burden to anyone. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself insisting that others do things your way because you believe there is a correct or best way to do them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no, I don't think I do that at all. I mean, I might offer a little suggestion here or there, but I certainly don't insist! \n\nI know some people get very particular about the \"right\" way to do things, but I just don't have the patience for that sort of back-and-forth. If someone else is doing a task, I just let them get on with it—or, well, if they're moving too slowly, I might just jump in and finish it myself rather than standing around arguing about how it ought to be done! I just can't stand standing still when there's work to be finished. \n\nI try my best to be agreeable, you know? I'd hate for my children to think I'm turning into a bossy old woman who won't let them do things their way. I really do try to just be helpful and keep things moving along. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not giving you the impression that I'm difficult to be around.\n\nDoctor: When you are away from your family, do you feel anxious or afraid that you won’t be able to cope on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I don't feel anxious at all when I'm away from them. \n\nI mean, I love my family dearly, of course, but I'm perfectly capable of managing on my own! I've been taking care of myself for a long time, and I really don't sit around worrying about whether I can cope. If I'm out and about, or running errands, I'm usually too busy buzzing from one thing to the next to even think about feeling afraid. I just keep moving! \n\nI really try to be strong and independent so my kids don't feel like they have to constantly check up on me. I hope that shows that I have a good, healthy mindset about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty starting things on your own unless someone else tells you to or approves first?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I mean, I certainly try to be polite about it—there's no need to be disagreeable just for the sake of it, right? \n\nBut if I really think someone is mistaken about something important, I'll usually just pipe up and say so. I suppose I'm a bit too quick to speak my mind sometimes, but I always mean well. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound argumentative... I really do try to be agreeable most of the time. Is it okay that I just speak up like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time feeling tense or worried, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: Oh, my, no. I really don't think I do. I don't really sit still long enough to let worry catch up with me, I suppose. I hope that's a good thing? I'm trying my very best to give you all the information you need... is that the sort of answer you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you often found it hard to do things on your own because you feel you need other people to take care of you?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no.
